    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I just don’t see how any of this gets fixed anymore. The studio, my life, all of it—it feels like no matter how hard I work, no matter what kind of payment plan somebody puts in front of me, I’m still just… stuck.\n\nI mean, I’ve worked with my hands my whole life, and usually if something’s crooked or broken, you can measure it, cut it right, fix it. But this doesn’t feel like that. I can’t see it getting back on track.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days. I wake up and it’s like this heavy gray thing is already there before I even get my feet on the floor.\n\nAnd it’s strange, because I’ll go into the shop—wood everywhere, tools, all the things I used to actually enjoy—and now it just feels… flat. Like I’m looking at someone else’s workbench. Sometimes I catch myself just standing there staring instead of doing anything, and I have to force myself to move.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like working in the shop or other hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. The shop used to be the one place where I could kind of lose myself, you know? The smell of the wood, getting a joint to fit just right… I used to care about all that.\n\nNow most days I wake up and that heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me, and even when I’m in there surrounded by all the wood and tools, it just feels flat. Sometimes I go in meaning to start something, and then I’m just standing there staring at the bench like I forgot why I came in. I have to really push myself just to move.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel especially tired or low on energy most days, even when you haven’t done much physically?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not even like I’ve been doing real heavy work first. By midmorning my arms already feel like I’ve been hauling lumber all day.\n\nAnd I keep thinking, well, maybe I just didn’t sleep enough, but even after I’ve been in bed all night, I’ll get to the shop and just measuring a board or making a cut feels like it takes everything. Even returning calls—just picking up the phone and dealing with that—I feel wiped out before I’m halfway through. It’s… embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating a lot less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of that. My wife’s probably better at noticing if my pants are fitting different or if I’m leaving food on the plate.\n\nI mean, some days I eat whatever’s easy, like standing at the counter, and then I forget what I even had. Other days I’ll go out to the truck and find an old sandwich wrapper from lunch and think, “When did I buy that?” But… weight-wise, appetite-wise, I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like a failure or blaming yourself for things that aren’t your fault lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. More than I probably should.\n\nLike, if I’m fitting a joint and it’s off just a hair, or I realize I missed getting a bid in on time, I don’t just think, “Okay, fix it” or “that was a mistake.” I go straight to, “You’re a lousy carpenter.” Like maybe I’ve been fooling myself this whole time.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about the studio, and how I wanted it to be this thing I could be proud of… I don’t know. I end up feeling like maybe I had no business trying to run it myself in the first place. It sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself thinking a lot about death or dying lately, or felt like life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t know how to answer that exactly.\n\nI mean, I think about getting older sometimes, like when my back hurts after work or I see guys I used to work with slowing down. There was this one fella I knew who retired and then didn’t know what to do with himself, just sat in his garage all day fixing the same lawnmower over and over… but that’s not really what you’re asking, I guess.\n\nAs far as death or thinking life isn’t worth living… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting there making some kind of plan or anything. It’s more like… I don’t know. Some days I just get tired of everything being so heavy. But I’m not sure if that counts as what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before—where you felt down, lost interest in things, and had trouble with energy—before this most recent one?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate it out, honestly.\n\nI mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, sure—everybody does, I guess. There were winters where work slowed down and I’d be sitting around sharpening chisels that didn’t need sharpening, just because I didn’t know what else to do with my hands. And then you get busy again and you tell yourself, “See, it was just the season.”\n\nBut if you mean, like… the same as now, with the whole package of it, I don’t know. I don’t keep a record of my moods or anything. My wife might say I’ve been like this off and on longer than I think. I just kind of keep going until somebody points it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time before when you felt down or lost interest in things for most of the day, nearly every day, and it lasted for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can pin it down that neatly… like “two weeks” and “most of the day.” I’m not great at tracking that kind of thing.\n\nThere’ve been times where I was just… low for a while, yeah, but then work picks up or there’s something at the house that needs doing, and I sort of stop paying attention to how long it’s been. Like if I’m building a set of cabinets, I can tell you exactly when I cut a rail wrong three months ago, but ask me how many days I felt off and I kind of draw a blank.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m not trying to dodge the question. I just don’t know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things for most of the day, nearly every day, and it lasted for at least two weeks without a break?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one clear stretch like that, but… I don’t know. It all kind of blurs together when I look back.\n\nThere were times I wasn’t myself for a while, sure, but whether it was “nearly every day” and no break for two weeks… I can’t honestly say. I’d go to work, come home, fix something around the house, maybe not feel much about any of it—but then there’d be a decent afternoon or some job that kept me moving, and I’d tell myself it didn’t count.\n\nSo I guess… I can’t recall a clean period like you’re describing. Not with enough certainty to say yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for a while—like weeks or months—before this most recent period?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably, if you’re saying “weeks or months” in the looser sense. There’ve been a few stretches in my life where I wasn’t really looking forward to anything, or I’d just kind of go through the motions.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to count them, you know? Like one time work was slow and I thought it was just because I didn’t have enough jobs lined up. Another time we had stuff going on at home, and I figured, well, anybody would feel lousy with that. I tend to explain it away.\n\nSo… more than once, maybe. I can’t give you a clean number or say exactly how long each one lasted.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things for most of the day, nearly every day, and it lasted for at least two weeks without a break?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, lately it’s been most days. I wake up and it’s like this heavy gray thing is already sitting on me before I’ve even done anything.\n\nAnd the shop used to be the place where I could kind of reset, you know? The smell of the wood, getting a cut just right… I could lose myself in that. Now I’ll be standing there surrounded by it, and it all just feels flat. Like I’m looking at somebody else’s workbench. Sometimes I have to really push myself not to just stand there staring.\n\nAs far as before this current stretch, I’m still not sure I can separate it out cleanly into “two weeks without a break.” It’s more like… that feeling has shown up plenty, but I don’t know if I tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back and confirm if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things most of the day, nearly every day, and it lasted for at least two weeks straight without getting better for a few days?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can confirm it that cleanly, like “two weeks straight” and no better days. I didn’t really keep track of it that way.\n\nWhat I can say is… most days now I wake up and it’s like that heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I even get moving. And in the shop—where I used to actually feel something, with the wood and the tools and getting into the work—it just feels flat. I’ll be standing there looking at the bench and have to fight not to just stand there staring.\n\nSo before this stretch, I know I’ve had that kind of feeling come and go, but I can’t honestly pin down a past time and say yes, it was every day for two weeks straight.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back and tell me if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things most of the day, nearly every day, and it lasted for at least two weeks without getting better for a few days?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking about before this current stretch, and I’m trying to think of it that way… but I keep coming back to how it is now.\n\nMost days I wake up and it’s already there, that heavy gray feeling, like it got up before I did. And even when I’m in the shop, surrounded by all the wood and tools—stuff I used to actually love being around—it just feels flat. I’ll be standing there at the bench and have to fight not to just stare at it.\n\nBefore now… I don’t know if I can honestly say it was exactly two weeks straight without a few better days. I remember feeling like this before, but I didn’t mark it off on a calendar or anything.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, can you clearly recall a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks straight without feeling better for even a couple of days?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to answer that exact thing, but I still can’t really separate out some earlier two-week block in my head. It all kind of blurs together.\n\nWhat I can say is what I’ve been saying—most days now I wake up and that heavy, gray feeling is already sitting on me. And I go into the shop, where I used to love the wood and the tools and just being around it, and it’s like… nothing. Flat. I’ll be standing there and have to fight not to just stare at the bench.\n\nBut looking back before this current stretch, no, I can’t clearly recall it in that exact way—two weeks straight without even a couple better days.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things most of the day, nearly every day, and it lasted for at least two weeks without a break?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking about before this current period, and I’m trying not to dodge it… I just don’t have it laid out in my head like that, like a clean two-week stretch I can point to.\n\nWhat I keep coming back to is how it feels most days now. I wake up and it’s like this heavy gray thing is already sitting on me before I even get out of bed. And at the shop—being around the wood, the tools, all of that used to feel like something to me. Now it’s just flat. Sometimes I’m standing there at the bench and I have to fight not to just stare at it.\n\nSo… before this, I can’t really say for sure whether there was a two-week period without a break. I remember the feeling, but not in a way I can mark off like that.\n\nDoctor: Looking back before this current period, can you clearly recall a time when you felt down or lost interest in things most of the day, nearly every day, and it lasted for at least two weeks straight without getting better for even a few days?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking about before this current period, and I’m trying to pin it down, but my head keeps sliding back to how it is now.\n\nMost days I wake up and it’s already there, that heavy gray feeling, like it’s been sitting on me all night. And then I get to the shop, and it’s strange because the smell of the wood used to be enough to kind of pull me in—different grain, different cuts, all that—and now I can be standing right in the middle of it and it just feels flat. Like none of it reaches me. Sometimes I’m at the bench and I have to fight not to just stand there staring.\n\nBefore this… I can’t clearly point to a clean two weeks like you’re asking.
